Validating LLM-Modernized Scientific Software Through Differential Fault Injection

Evan Coleman, Yuzhong Shen, Masha Sosonkina et al.. Validating LLM-Modernized Scientific Software Through Differential Fault Injection. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates whether large language model agents can reliably modernize legacy Fortran scientific software while preserving not just nominal behavior but also fault-response characteristics. The authors developed a differential fault-injection validation framework that instruments a shared computational driver and applies identical, deterministic faults to both original and modernized code, allowing the two implementations to be compared under stress conditions rather than only under normal execution. Testing was conducted on the self-consistent-field driver of a quantum chemistry package, targeting integral kernels converted by the language model, across thousands of injection runs covering transient faults, persistent perturbations, and reduced-precision scenarios. The experiments revealed phase-dependent parallel deadlocks and false convergence under reduced precision, while demonstrating that the modernized kernels behaved identically to the originals across all paired injection trials. The work establishes differential fault injection as a principled complement to nominal testing for validating LLM-driven code modernization, and shows that additional synchronization fixes compose cleanly with the modernization without introducing divergence.

The Okinawa Lectures on Entropy

Klaas Landsman. The Okinawa Lectures on Entropy. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper presents a comprehensive lecture course on entropy spanning both classical and quantum settings, aimed at readers with backgrounds in mathematics and theoretical physics. The classical treatment develops entropy through large deviation theory, connecting foundational results to both Boltzmannian and Gibbsian statistical mechanics. On the quantum side, the course traces how quantum entropies relate to their classical counterparts both formally and more deeply through their role in statistical hypothesis testing, covering the Kullback-Leibler relative entropy, its quantum generalization due to Umegaki, and Rényi deformations within this unified framework. The course then addresses the substantially harder problem of defining relative entropy for states on subsystems described by von Neumann algebras, requiring modular theory and Haagerup's noncommutative function spaces to recover and extend the constructions of Araki and Uhlmann. Finally, the course introduces a quantum analogue of the Kolmogorov-Sinai dynamical entropy in the von Neumann algebraic setting, with the entire treatment motivated by potential relevance to black hole thermodynamics while remaining mathematically rigorous throughout.

RegRole: Regularized Role Detection and Prediction in Temporal Dynamic Networks

Emily J Evans, Weihong Guo, Carlotta Domenicon. RegRole: Regularized Role Detection and Prediction in Temporal Dynamic Networks. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses the challenge of identifying and tracking roles that network nodes play as graph structure evolves over time. The authors developed a dynamic role discovery method based on temporally regularized Non-negative Matrix Factorization, which enforces consistency in role definitions across time steps and penalizes abrupt changes in role membership to improve robustness against noise. Unlike prior approaches, the method produces a single unified set of roles valid across all time periods alongside a universal transition matrix describing the likelihood of moving between roles. The approach was evaluated on multiple real-world and synthetic datasets using both engineered and automatically generated node features, demonstrating reduced role prediction errors compared to competing techniques. Analysis of the transition matrices further showed that the learned system is more stable, with nodes tending to remain in their roles rather than switching arbitrarily, and the method scales efficiently to large, sparse graphs.

Twin: Playing an Unknown Game with a Test-Time Digital Twin

Alexy Skoutnev, Kirill Acharya, Gaston Longhitano et al.. Twin: Playing an Unknown Game with a Test-Time Digital Twin. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper introduces Twin (Test-time World-model Inference), a system designed to play unknown grid games by having a frontier coding agent construct an executable world model purely through simulation and interaction, without any hand-engineered task-specific designs. The system works by requiring that a world model accurately reproduce every previously observed game transition before any action is taken, using mismatches between predictions and actual outcomes as counterexamples to iteratively repair the model. Twin was evaluated on a continual learning benchmark of grid games and achieved near-complete success, clearing the vast majority of levels with efficiency exceeding that of first-time human players on most of them. A key finding was that the system could infer the game's goal before receiving any reward signal on the majority of cleared levels, with the remaining cases handled through automated search. The authors conclude that constructing a usable world model is more tractable than anticipated, while inferring the correct goal representation emerges as the more challenging subproblem.

Isomorphism of tournaments with bounded VC dimension

Simon Raßmann, Pascal Schweitzer. Isomorphism of tournaments with bounded VC dimension. arXiv. 2026 preprint

The paper investigates the computational complexity of deciding whether two tournaments are isomorphic, a problem recognized as a key bottleneck in the broader graph isomorphism problem. The authors focus on tournaments of bounded VC dimension, a class of combinatorial structures for which neither hardness nor polynomial-time algorithms were previously known, and resolve an open problem by providing a quasi-polynomial algorithm whose running time is polynomial in the exponent with respect to the VC dimension parameter. To achieve this, they develop an isomorphism-invariant decomposition method for tournaments, introduce the concept of patched tournaments to enable recursion, and exploit the structure of near twins to balance the size and number of decomposed pieces. As a complementary result, they also establish that tournament isomorphism is polynomial-time decidable for tournaments of bounded chromatic number, addressing the class that characterizes unbounded VC dimension in hereditary tournament families. Together, these results significantly advance the understanding of tractable cases for tournament isomorphism.

Geometry-Conforming Finite Element Methods for Interface Problems on Fitted and Unfitted Meshes

Yuanhui Lin, Tao Lin, Xu Zhang et al.. Geometry-Conforming Finite Element Methods for Interface Problems on Fitted and Unfitted Meshes. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper develops a geometry-conforming finite element framework for solving elliptic boundary value and interface problems on two-dimensional curved domains. The approach uses the Frenet–Serret transformation to represent curved boundaries and interfaces exactly, generating nonpolynomial local shape functions in physical coordinates that conform to the prescribed geometry without approximation. For meshes that do not align with interfaces, the framework couples geometry-conforming spaces on curved-boundary elements with geometry-conforming immersed finite element spaces on interface-cut elements. The authors establish rigorous theoretical results including optimal approximation, inverse, and trace estimates, as well as well-posedness and optimal energy and norm error estimates for a symmetric interior penalty discontinuous Galerkin discretization on fitted meshes. Numerical experiments confirm the predicted convergence rates and demonstrate that the method avoids the geometric variational crime inherent in conventional curved-geometry approximation, achieving accuracy comparable to isoparametric finite elements while yielding smaller interface trace errors in reported tests.

Respiratory multi-viral rapid-antigen point-of-care tests in New Zealand primary care: a mixed-methods pilot.

Bibby S, Marsh S, Dowell A et al.. Respiratory multi-viral rapid-antigen point-of-care tests in New Zealand primary care: a mixed-methods pilot.. BJGP open. 2026

This study examined whether multi-viral rapid-antigen point-of-care tests, capable of detecting multiple respiratory viruses simultaneously, could be feasibly integrated into routine primary care in New Zealand. A prospective mixed-methods pilot was conducted across seven primary care clinics during winter, combining electronic medical record audits with surveys, interviews, and focus groups involving both patients and clinicians. The tests were administered to patients presenting with acute respiratory symptoms as a broader alternative to COVID-19-only testing, and outcomes related to acceptability and clinical utility were assessed. Both clinicians and patients responded positively to the tests, with all clinicians finding them useful in practice and a majority reporting that results influenced antibiotic prescribing decisions. The authors conclude that these tests were readily incorporated into primary care workflows and may improve management of common respiratory illnesses while encouraging patients who test positive to adopt transmission-reducing behaviours.

Evaluation of the QuickFinder COVID-19/Flu/RSV antigen test for rapid simultaneous detection of SARS-CoV-2, influenza A/B viruses, and respiratory syncytial virus.

Kim JA, Kang M, Yu HJ et al.. Evaluation of the QuickFinder COVID-19/Flu/RSV antigen test for rapid simultaneous detection of SARS-CoV-2, influenza A/B viruses, and respiratory syncytial virus.. Diagnostic microbiology and infectious disease. 2026

This study evaluated a lateral flow immunoassay-based antigen rapid diagnostic test capable of simultaneously detecting SARS-CoV-2, influenza A and B viruses, and respiratory syncytial virus from nasopharyngeal swab specimens. The test's performance was assessed against real-time reverse transcription polymerase chain reaction as a reference standard using archived specimens, and cross-reactivity was examined against a panel of respiratory viral and bacterial pathogens. The test demonstrated high sensitivity across all four viral targets in specimens with high viral loads and achieved perfect specificity with no cross-reactivity observed against any of the tested microorganisms. Sensitivity was reduced in specimens with low viral loads, leading the authors to conclude that the test is reliable as a rapid screening and initial differentiation tool but should complement rather than replace molecular testing.

No detection of respiratory syncytial virus at one clinic in Japan with rapid immunochromatographic antigen tests for three viruses using the same nasopharyngeal swabs from adult patients

Kajiwara N, Tsunetoshi Y, Shiode S. No detection of respiratory syncytial virus at one clinic in Japan with rapid immunochromatographic antigen tests for three viruses using the same nasopharyngeal swabs from adult patients. 2026

This study investigated the disease burden of respiratory syncytial virus infection in adult patients at a Japanese clinic by testing nasopharyngeal swabs collected from individuals presenting with acute respiratory infection symptoms. Samples were analyzed using rapid immunochromatographic antigen tests capable of detecting SARS-CoV-2, influenza A and B viruses, and RSV simultaneously. While cases of SARS-CoV-2 and influenza A were detected with reasonable frequency and one influenza B case was identified, no RSV-positive cases were found among the patients tested. Comparison with prior studies that employed polymerase chain reaction testing revealed a statistically significant difference in RSV detection rates, with PCR identifying RSV in a notable proportion of adult patients. The authors conclude that rapid immunochromatographic antigen testing has limited utility for diagnosing RSV infections in Japanese adults, likely due to insufficient sensitivity compared to PCR-based methods.

Performance of an antigen test for detection of SARS-CoV-2, influenza, and respiratory syncytial virus-a prospective study.

Berger K, Vang SH, Bekkeseth RHO et al.. Performance of an antigen test for detection of SARS-CoV-2, influenza, and respiratory syncytial virus-a prospective study.. Clinical microbiology and infection : the official publication of the European Society of Clinical Microbiology and Infectious Diseases. 2026

This study evaluated the diagnostic performance of a multiplex rapid antigen test capable of simultaneously detecting SARS-CoV-2, influenza A, influenza B, and respiratory syncytial virus in patients presenting with acute respiratory symptoms. The test demonstrated high sensitivity for all four viral targets when using a PCR cycle threshold cutoff intended to reflect infectious viral loads, along with perfect specificity across all agents and age groups. A notable finding was that nasal swabs absorbed less viral RNA than nasopharyngeal swabs, a difference that reached statistical significance for influenza A in the broader patient population but not in children. The authors conclude that the test's performance supports its potential utility as a marker of infectiousness in acute clinical care settings.

Can exercise affect immune function to increase susceptibility to infection?

Richard J. Simpson, Richard J. Simpson, Maree Gleeson et al.. Can exercise affect immune function to increase susceptibility to infection?. PubMed. 2026

This debate article examined the long-standing question in exercise immunology of whether exercise itself can suppress immune function and increase susceptibility to infection. The paper contrasted the generally accepted benefits of regular moderate-intensity exercise for immune defense with the high infection burden reported among elite athletes and military personnel performing arduous physical activity. Opposing expert groups debated key contested issues, including whether athletes are genuinely more infection-prone than the general population, whether exercise independently drives increased infection risk apart from confounding factors such as sleep disruption, anxiety, nutritional deficits, and travel, and whether current biomarkers and study designs are adequate to address these questions. Both groups agreed that infection susceptibility is multifactorial in origin, but could not resolve whether exercise per se is a direct causative factor in elevated infection risk among those performing high-intensity training. The authors conclude that the debate highlights a need for more rigorous empirical research to disentangle the specific contribution of exercise from the many coexisting variables in these populations.

Spinal cord injury: Known and possible influences on the immune response to exercise

Christof A. Leicht, Victoria L. Goosey‐Tolfrey, Nicolette C. Bishop. Spinal cord injury: Known and possible influences on the immune response to exercise. PubMed. 2026

This review examined how spinal cord injury affects immune function, with particular focus on the immune response to exercise. The authors conducted a comprehensive literature search across multiple databases to gather existing evidence from patient, recreationally active, and athlete populations with spinal cord injury. Spinal cord injury disrupts autonomic function and alters catecholamine concentrations at rest and following exercise, which likely directly suppresses immune cells given that the vast majority of lymphoid cells express adrenergic receptors, with these effects being especially pronounced in individuals with tetraplegia. However, some immune responses to exercise, such as increases in natural killer cell cytotoxicity and mucosal immunity changes in those with tetraplegia, appear independent of catecholamine alterations and may resemble responses seen in able-bodied individuals, suggesting compensatory mechanisms such as sympathetic reflex activity, receptor hypersensitivity, or neuroendocrine adjustments may offset injury-related immune deficits. The authors conclude that exercise and rehabilitation therapy show promise as tools to maintain or improve immune and autonomic function following spinal cord injury, and call for further research to clarify the underlying mechanisms and their implications for infection susceptibility.

Osteoarthritis: molecular pathogenesis and potential therapeutic options

Yi Zhang, Yanqi Han, Ying Sun et al.. Osteoarthritis: molecular pathogenesis and potential therapeutic options. Signal Transduction and Targeted Therapy. 2026

This review examines the molecular mechanisms underlying osteoarthritis, a chronic joint disorder characterized by pain, inflammation, and bone deterioration, with the goal of identifying more effective therapeutic targets. The authors synthesized current knowledge on nociceptive pathways connecting the knee joint to the central nervous system, as well as pathological changes in bone and cartilage driven by inflammatory cytokines and organelle dysfunction. They highlight three key intracellular processes that drive disease progression: mitochondrial dysfunction with excessive reactive oxygen species production, lysosomal destabilization leading to chondrocyte apoptosis, and unresolved endoplasmic reticulum stress that exacerbates cartilage degradation. Based on these mechanistic insights, the review proposes a multimodal therapeutic framework targeting inflammatory cascades, organelle homeostasis restoration through approaches such as mitophagy enhancement and lysosomal integrity preservation, and nociceptive signaling attenuation. The authors also emphasize the potential of organelle-targeted drug delivery systems to improve the efficacy and stability of these emerging treatments.

Bispecific T cell engagers for treatment-refractory autoimmune connective tissue diseases

Christina Düsing, Andrea‐Hermina Györfi, Ayla Nadja Stütz et al.. Bispecific T cell engagers for treatment-refractory autoimmune connective tissue diseases. Nature Medicine. 2026

This study investigated the use of bispecific T cell engager (TCE) therapies in patients with severe, treatment-refractory autoimmune connective tissue diseases, specifically antisynthetase syndrome and systemic sclerosis. Under compassionate use, patients with antisynthetase syndrome received the CD19×CD3 TCE blinatumomab, while patients with systemic sclerosis received the BCMA×CD3 TCE teclistamab, with both groups subsequently receiving rituximab as maintenance therapy to inhibit B cell redifferentiation. Both TCEs reduced their respective target cells in affected tissues and lowered autoantibody titers, with blinatumomab producing clinical, serological, and histological improvements in myositis and stabilization of interstitial lung disease, and teclistamab improving skin fibrosis, stabilizing interstitial lung disease, and resolving tendon friction rubs. Notably, maintenance rituximab achieved prolonged disease control even in patients who had previously failed rituximab therapy alone. Treatment was associated with adverse events including cytokine release syndrome up to grade 3 and respiratory infections, though no neurotoxicity syndrome was observed, suggesting these agents may serve as potential rescue therapies for refractory disease.

CPI-Bench: A Comprehensive,Practical and Intelligent Benchmark for Real-World Image Editing

Qinye Zhou, Jun Zheng, Yongchao Du et al.. CPI-Bench: A Comprehensive,Practical and Intelligent Benchmark for Real-World Image Editing. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses the limitations of existing image editing benchmarks, which are restricted to simple single-image tasks and fail to distinguish performance across diverse models in complex, real-world settings. The authors developed CPI-Bench, a new benchmark comprising three subsets targeting general editing diversity (including multi-image tasks), high-frequency practical user scenarios, and reasoning-intensive editing instructions. Evaluations of mainstream image editing models using this benchmark demonstrated improved differentiation between models across general, practical, and reasoning-based editing capabilities. Ranking analysis further showed that CPI-Bench achieves the strongest alignment with a human-preference leaderboard, suggesting it reliably reflects real-world user judgments and can serve as a robust proxy for human perceptual evaluation.

Validating LLM-Modernized Scientific Software Through Differential Fault Injection

Evan Coleman, Yuzhong Shen, Masha Sosonkina et al.. Validating LLM-Modernized Scientific Software Through Differential Fault Injection. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates whether large language model agents can reliably modernize legacy Fortran scientific software while preserving not just nominal behavior but also fault tolerance and numerical robustness. The researchers developed a differential fault-injection validation framework that applies identical, deterministic perturbations to both original and LLM-modernized code at multiple instrumentation sites within a shared computational driver, isolating the converted components for comparison. Testing focused on integral kernels within a quantum chemistry software package, examining responses to transient faults, persistent perturbations, and reduced floating-point precision across thousands of runs. The fault injection campaigns revealed phase-dependent parallel deadlocks and false convergence under reduced precision, while also producing quantitative models of how fault absorption costs scale with the number of corrupted bits. The original and modernized kernels agreed across all paired injection experiments, and a synchronization fix motivated by the campaign's findings also composed correctly with the modernization, supporting the method's utility for validating LLM-driven code transformations beyond simple correctness checks.

The Okinawa Lectures on Entropy

Klaas Landsman. The Okinawa Lectures on Entropy. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper presents a set of lecture notes providing a comprehensive treatment of entropy in both classical and quantum settings, aimed at bridging mathematical rigor with physical intuition. The classical theory is developed through the lens of large deviation theory, connecting foundational results to Boltzmannian and Gibbsian statistical physics, while quantum entropies are motivated through their deep role in statistical hypothesis testing. The treatment covers a progression from familiar Kullback-Leibler and Umegaki relative entropies to their Rényi deformations, and then to the more abstract setting of von Neumann algebras where Araki and Uhlmann relative entropies are defined using modular theory. Haagerup's noncommutative spaces are introduced to make these abstract quantum entropies tractable in terms of density operators and traces, and the Connes-Størmer-Narnhofer-Thirring entropy is presented as a quantum analogue of the Kolmogorov-Sinai entropy from ergodic theory. The course was motivated by connections to black hole thermodynamics, though that application itself is not covered, and it is designed to be accessible to readers with backgrounds in probability theory, functional analysis, and quantum theory.

RegRole: Regularized Role Detection and Prediction in Temporal Dynamic Networks

Emily J Evans, Weihong Guo, Carlotta Domenicon. RegRole: Regularized Role Detection and Prediction in Temporal Dynamic Networks. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses the problem of discovering and tracking roles that network nodes occupy as graph structures evolve over time. The authors developed a dynamic role detection method based on temporally regularized Non-negative Matrix Factorization, which enforces consistency of role definitions across time periods and penalizes abrupt changes in role membership to handle real-world noise. A key contribution is the derivation of a universal transition matrix describing the probability of nodes moving between roles over time, rather than producing role sets that vary independently at each time step. The method was evaluated on multiple real-world and synthetic datasets using both engineered and automatically generated node features. Results show that appropriate regularization reduces prediction error compared to competing techniques, and transition matrix analysis confirms that the approach produces more stable role assignments with fewer arbitrary node transitions.

Twin: Playing an Unknown Game with a Test-Time Digital Twin

Alexy Skoutnev, Kirill Acharya, Gaston Longhitano et al.. Twin: Playing an Unknown Game with a Test-Time Digital Twin. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper introduces Twin, a Test-time World-model Inference system designed to play unknown grid games by constructing an executable world model entirely from simulation and interaction, without any hand-engineered rules. A frontier coding agent writes and continuously repairs this world model, enforcing that it correctly reproduces every previously observed game transition before any action is taken, with mismatches used as counterexamples to refine the model. The system was evaluated on a continual learning benchmark of grid games similar to ARC-AGI-3, where the rules and goals are hidden from the player. Twin successfully cleared nearly all levels with high action efficiency compared to human players, and was able to infer the correct goal before receiving any reward on the majority of levels it completed. The authors conclude that constructing a usable world model is more tractable than expected, but that inferring the correct goal from interaction alone represents the harder and more fundamental challenge.

Isomorphism of tournaments with bounded VC dimension

Simon Raßmann, Pascal Schweitzer. Isomorphism of tournaments with bounded VC dimension. arXiv. 2026 preprint

The paper investigates the computational complexity of determining whether two tournaments (complete directed graphs) are isomorphic, a problem that has resisted classification for decades and represents a key bottleneck in graph isomorphism research. The authors resolve an open problem by proving that tournament isomorphism can be decided in quasi-polynomial time when the tournaments have bounded VC dimension, a central measure of combinatorial complexity. To achieve this, they develop a new isomorphism-invariant decomposition method for tournaments, introduce the concept of a "patched tournament" to enable recursion, and design a recursive algorithm that exploits near-twin structure while balancing piece size against piece count. As a consequence, automorphism groups of tournaments with bounded VC dimension can be computed in polynomial time. In a complementary result, the authors also establish that tournament isomorphism is polynomial-time decidable for tournaments of bounded chromatic number, addressing the class of tournaments that characterizes unbounded VC dimension via hereditary closure.

Biosensing technologies for foodborne pathogen detection and healthcare: principles, emerging materials, and intelligent platforms

Purshottam J. Assudani, Balakrishnan . P., Anny Leema A et al.. Biosensing technologies for foodborne pathogen detection and healthcare: principles, emerging materials, and intelligent platforms. Microchimica Acta. 2026

This review examines the current state and future trajectory of biosensing technologies designed to detect high-priority foodborne pathogens, with an emphasis on platforms suitable for clinical and field deployment. The authors survey electrochemical, optical, and microfluidic sensing modalities, exploring how advances in functional nanomaterials, aptamers, nanozymes, and surface engineering have improved sensor sensitivity, specificity, and performance in complex food matrices. A central theme is the integration of artificial intelligence and machine learning into biosensor systems, enabling real-time analytics, intelligent pattern recognition, and high-throughput multiplexed detection that transforms conventional instruments into smart diagnostic devices. The review critically addresses persistent obstacles such as matrix interference, bioreceptor instability, manufacturing scalability, and regulatory standardization, proposing mitigation strategies including ratiometric sensing, microfluidic pre-treatment, and explainable AI frameworks. Looking forward, the authors advocate for self-powered biosensors, modular pathogen panel hubs, blockchain-based traceability, and standardized validation pipelines as pathways toward regulation-ready platforms for next-generation food safety monitoring.

Advances in tuberculosis diagnostics and biomarkers, 2020-26: scientific evidence, recommendations, gaps, and developmental priorities.

Walzl G, McHugh TD, Chakaya J et al.. Advances in tuberculosis diagnostics and biomarkers, 2020-26: scientific evidence, recommendations, gaps, and developmental priorities.. The Lancet. Microbe. 2027

This paper reviews advances in tuberculosis diagnostics and biomarker research over a five-year period, summarizing how emerging evidence has shaped updated WHO policy guidance that consolidates recommendations for detecting tuberculosis disease and drug resistance into a single framework. The review covers developments across screening, diagnosis, and drug-resistance testing, including the expanded role of digital chest radiography with computer-aided detection as a frontline screening tool and the diversification of molecular diagnostic platforms enabling more decentralised testing. Progress in testing non-sputum specimen types such as stool, gastric aspirate, nasopharyngeal samples, and tongue swabs has improved bacteriological confirmation in children and other populations unable to produce sputum, while advances in rapid molecular drug-resistance testing and next-generation sequencing have strengthened reference laboratory capacity. Urine lipoarabinomannan assays offer diagnostic benefits for people living with HIV who have advanced disease, though performance limitations restrict broader application. Despite these gains, the review identifies persistent performance, implementation, and equity gaps, and notes that while host transcriptomic and other biomarker signatures show promise for prognosis and triage, no biomarker yet meets WHO criteria for a point-of-care test capable of reliably distinguishing tuberculosis infection from disease, including subclinical forms.

SARS-CoV-2 Point-of-Care Testing Modalities: Integrating Molecular, Immunological, Biosensor, and AI Approaches.

Hetta HF, Ahmed R, Haseeb A et al.. SARS-CoV-2 Point-of-Care Testing Modalities: Integrating Molecular, Immunological, Biosensor, and AI Approaches.. Diagnostics (Basel, Switzerland). 2026

This review examined the landscape of point-of-care testing technologies developed for SARS-CoV-2 diagnosis, motivated by the urgent diagnostic demands exposed by the COVID-19 pandemic. The authors conducted a structured literature search across major databases and synthesized evidence on molecular assays, antigen and antibody tests, biosensor platforms, and artificial intelligence-assisted diagnostic approaches. Molecular methods such as rapid RT-PCR, LAMP, and CRISPR-based technologies were found to offer high sensitivity and specificity suited to decentralized settings, while antigen tests provide rapid and affordable screening despite variable performance depending on viral load and circulating variants. Antibody-based assays were identified as more appropriate for seroprevalence and retrospective applications than for acute infection detection, and emerging biosensor and AI tools showed promise but remain largely at prototype or early-validation stages requiring further clinical evidence. The review concludes that no single testing modality is universally optimal, and that complementary deployment of these approaches, alongside advances in multiplexing, digital connectivity, and variant-resilient design, will be essential for both COVID-19 management and broader pandemic preparedness.

AI-Enhanced Point-of-Care Diagnostics for Infectious Diseases in Resource-Limited Settings: A Scoping Review.

Farquhar H. AI-Enhanced Point-of-Care Diagnostics for Infectious Diseases in Resource-Limited Settings: A Scoping Review.. Tropical medicine & international health : TM & IH. 2026

This scoping review systematically mapped the landscape of research combining artificial intelligence with point-of-care and rapid diagnostic technologies for infectious diseases in resource-limited settings. Following established scoping review methodology, the authors searched five major databases covering publications from early 2015 through early 2026, screening over a thousand records and ultimately including more than two hundred studies. The field showed exponential growth in publication volume, with the majority of work concentrated on COVID-19, malaria, and tuberculosis, while neglected tropical diseases were markedly underrepresented, and convolutional neural networks were the dominant analytical approach across imaging-based modalities such as microscopy and biosensors. Geographically, research was heavily concentrated in East Africa and South Asia, leaving West Africa and Latin America largely understudied. The authors identified a critical gap in research rigour, finding that the vast majority of studies lacked prospective field validation, and they call for future work to prioritise real-world validation, broaden disease coverage beyond the dominant triad, and meaningfully involve end-user communities from the earliest stages of development.

An integrated centrifugal microfluidic CRISPR-based diagnostics platform for multiplexed point-of-care testing of respiratory pathogens.

Li L, Wei H, Wei M et al.. An integrated centrifugal microfluidic CRISPR-based diagnostics platform for multiplexed point-of-care testing of respiratory pathogens.. Biosensors & bioelectronics. 2026

The paper developed and validated a centrifugal microfluidic diagnostic platform called iCARD for rapid multiplexed detection of respiratory pathogens at the point of care. The system integrates CRISPR/Cas13a chemistry with lyophilized reagents pre-loaded onto a spinning disc and a portable fluorescence analyzer, enabling automated processing after sample loading, along with a novel resin-based method to purify and concentrate nucleic acids from throat swabs. The platform can simultaneously screen six respiratory pathogens across four independent samples in under an hour, achieving single-copy analytical sensitivity without cross-reactivity between targets. Clinical validation using retrospective patient throat swabs demonstrated very high diagnostic sensitivity and perfect specificity, supporting the platform's accuracy for decentralized testing. The authors conclude that iCARD represents a robust and high-throughput molecular diagnostic tool suitable for point-of-care deployment and epidemiological surveillance.

Point-of-care testing (POCT) for Infectious diseases: technological innovation, effectiveness evaluation, and application strategies in resource-limited regions.

Zhang YX, Ru JF. Point-of-care testing (POCT) for Infectious diseases: technological innovation, effectiveness evaluation, and application strategies in resource-limited regions.. Diagnostic microbiology and infectious disease. 2026

This review examines point-of-care testing technologies for infectious diseases, focusing on their role in early screening, epidemic surveillance, and public health prevention. The authors systematically surveyed recent advances across molecular diagnostics, microfluidic systems, biosensing technologies, and artificial intelligence as applied to rapid diagnostic platforms. They also analyzed the quality assessment frameworks used to evaluate these technologies, including key performance indicators such as sensitivity, specificity, detection limits, stability, and field applicability. Particular emphasis is placed on deployment strategies for resource-limited settings, addressing technical optimization, resource allocation models, and local support infrastructure. The review concludes by identifying current challenges and proposing future development directions to guide further research and implementation of infectious disease point-of-care testing.

Recent advances in Strongyloides screening, diagnostics, therapeutics, and management.

Clark EH, Nutman TB. Recent advances in Strongyloides screening, diagnostics, therapeutics, and management.. Current opinion in infectious diseases. 2026

The paper reviews recent advances in the screening, diagnosis, treatment, and management of strongyloidiasis, a parasitic infection that remains widely underdiagnosed despite its potentially fatal consequences in immunocompromised individuals. On the diagnostic front, molecular methods using nucleic acid amplification have superseded traditional parasitologic approaches, and new rapid diagnostic tests based on recombinant antigens have improved point-of-care serologic screening compared to standard immunoglobulin-based assays. Treatment options are expanding, with moxidectin demonstrating non-inferiority to the established standard ivermectin, and emodepside showing promising cure rates in early trials through a mechanistically distinct mode of action. The World Health Organization has conditionally recommended mass drug administration of ivermectin in highly endemic communities, and updated screening recommendations now reflect a broader understanding of which immunosuppressed populations face the greatest risk of severe hyperinfection. The authors conclude that while diagnostic and therapeutic tools are improving, harmonized screening algorithms and prospective studies in high-risk groups remain critical priorities given limited data in severe disease and special populations.

EULAR recommendations for the management of rheumatoid arthritis with synthetic and biologic disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs: 2025 update

Josef S. Smolen, Christopher J. Edwards, Victoria Konzett et al.. EULAR recommendations for the management of rheumatoid arthritis with synthetic and biologic disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs: 2025 update. Annals of the Rheumatic Diseases. 2026

This paper presents the updated recommendations from a major European rheumatology alliance for managing rheumatoid arthritis using synthetic and biologic disease-modifying antirheumatic drugs. An international task force conducted systematic literature reviews on the efficacy and safety of these therapies, then used a structured voting process to revise and consolidate guidance from the previous update. The resulting framework was streamlined to fewer recommendations organized around overarching principles covering conventional synthetic, biological, and targeted synthetic agents, including Janus kinase inhibitors. The recommendations endorse starting with methotrexate combined with short-term glucocorticoids, escalating to a biologic agent after an inadequate response, and considering Janus kinase inhibitors with careful attention to risks such as major cardiovascular events, malignancies, and thromboembolic events. Guidance is also provided on treat-to-target strategies, sequencing after treatment failure, and cautious tapering in sustained remission, with high levels of evidence and agreement supporting most recommendations.

Smart wearable and implantable biosensors for continuous health monitoring: materials, biocompatibility, and AI integration

Thirumalaisamy Suryaprabha, Chunghyeon Choi, Yanfang Wu et al.. Smart wearable and implantable biosensors for continuous health monitoring: materials, biocompatibility, and AI integration. npj Flexible Electronics. 2026

This review examines the current state of smart wearable and implantable biosensors designed for continuous, real-time monitoring of biophysical and biochemical signals in support of personalized and preventive healthcare. The paper surveys advances in flexible, stretchable, and biocompatible materials that allow long-term wear and seamless integration with the body, alongside multimodal sensing approaches that improve measurement robustness. The role of artificial intelligence in enhancing signal processing and generating predictive health insights is also assessed. Despite notable progress, the review identifies persistent challenges including motion artifacts, energy autonomy, data privacy, and the clinical interpretability of sensor outputs. The authors synthesize findings across materials science, device architecture, and AI-assisted strategies to provide a comprehensive overview of the field.

Food and Medicine Homology Focus in 2026

Mengyao Li, Gui-Fen Wu, Feng Lu et al.. Food and Medicine Homology Focus in 2026. Food & medicine homology.. 2026

This perspective paper examines the anticipated trajectory of the food and medicine homology field as it transitions from traditional empirical knowledge toward contemporary scientific frameworks. The authors outline a vision centered on scientific rigor, mechanistic understanding, precision medicine principles, and modernization of the industrial chain through advanced technologies. Key research directions identified include active component identification, multisystem interaction mechanisms involving gut microbiota, and emerging biological processes such as phase separation and immunometabolic reprogramming. Applications are expected to shift toward dynamic, personalized health management using multidimensional data and artificial intelligence to address chronic diseases and aging populations. The paper also anticipates industrial transformation through synthetic biology, intelligent delivery systems, and sustainable manufacturing practices to create a more transparent and evidence-based ecosystem.

Split the Labor: Separating Evidence Interpretation from Decision Aggregation

Zhelun Wu. Split the Labor: Separating Evidence Interpretation from Decision Aggregation. arXiv. 2026 preprint

The paper argues that systems using language models to draw conclusions from multiple sources make a design error by conflating two fundamentally distinct operations: interpreting individual sources and aggregating those interpretations into a final decision. The authors propose separating these operations and define a structured four-field evidence tuple as the interface between them, which they argue fully determines the design of both components. They identify a failure mode they term count-scale drift, wherein summing unnormalized weights causes the effective decision threshold to shift with the number of sources consulted, and show that the vote rule and posterior ordering diverge when source reliabilities differ, with no single threshold able to reconcile them. Pooling calibrated log-likelihood ratios is presented as an arithmetic rather than architectural fix that resolves both problems and generalizes beyond language models to any score-summing system. The framework is validated on a longitudinal corpus in both retrospective and prospective settings, where the proposed separation yields strong predictive performance on a survival task, and the authors explicitly enumerate falsifiable predictions, negative results, and remaining confounds.

Residual-Controlled Douglas--Rachford Splitting for Differentiable Solver Layers

Kang Liu, Jianchen Hu. Residual-Controlled Douglas--Rachford Splitting for Differentiable Solver Layers. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper addresses the challenge of embedding constrained optimization solvers into end-to-end learned systems, where fixed-depth unrolling forces difficult trade-offs among solution quality, feasibility, and computational cost. The authors propose Residual-Controlled Douglas–Rachford Splitting, a differentiable solver layer for conic linear programs that treats the unrolled solver as a feedback-controlled dynamical system, using a causal controller to adaptively tune relaxation and objective-drive parameters while preserving the projection-splitting structure of Douglas–Rachford splitting. Theoretically, they establish that each admissible block remains an averaged relaxed operator and admits finite-step fixed-point residual bounds, and they analyze time-varying rollouts as summable perturbations of a limiting averaged operator to recover primal-dual diagnostics from the final solver state. Experiments on mixed-cone benchmarks and engineering applications demonstrate that the proposed method improves solution quality, feasibility, and downstream decision performance compared to fixed-depth unrolling approaches.

PACE-Bench: Benchmarking Physics Adaptation via Code Evolution in Dynamic Environments

Yuhao Zhan, Bingxiang He, Zecong Tang et al.. PACE-Bench: Benchmarking Physics Adaptation via Code Evolution in Dynamic Environments. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper introduces a benchmark designed to evaluate whether self-evolving AI agents can adapt code-based physical designs when the underlying physics of their environment changes. The benchmark consists of source-to-target adaptation pairs spanning six physics domains, where a design that succeeds under source conditions must be iteratively revised to work under mutated target conditions using sandbox feedback within a limited attempt budget. Ten self-evolving methods representing four paradigms were compared, revealing that the benchmark is far from solved and that simulator-grounded reflection outperforms unverified self-revision, while memory mechanisms anchor agents to early designs and broad tree search fails to converge. Notably, providing agents with exact information about the physical changes did not substantially raise performance, suggesting that the core challenge is mechanism redesign rather than parameter inference. These findings highlight fundamental limitations in current self-evolving agents when faced with dynamic environmental conditions requiring structural adaptation rather than incremental refinement.

Local and Global Regimes of Geometric Complexity in Language Model Representations

Arwa Osman, Marco Baroni, Iuri Macocco. Local and Global Regimes of Geometric Complexity in Language Model Representations. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper investigates how intrinsic dimensionality, a common measure of representational complexity in language models, is affected by properties of datasets rather than language itself. The researchers focused specifically on lexical diversity, defined as the number of unique final tokens in a dataset, and examined how it influences intrinsic dimensionality estimates across different scales. They discovered a scale-dependent transition between two distinct regimes: at low lexical diversity, fewer unique final words paradoxically yield higher intrinsic dimensionality, while at high lexical diversity this relationship reverses. The researchers derived an exact, parameter-free formula that accurately predicts the transition point between these two regimes at every scale tested. Their findings caution against straightforward interpretation of intrinsic dimensionality as a reflection of representational complexity, while also revealing a general organizational principle governing how linguistic data is structured within language model representations.

TRIAGE: Risk-Controlled Pseudo-Label Admission for Annotation-Efficient Semi-Supervised Retinal OCT Classification

Md Ashraful Hossen Akash, Shyla Afroge, Abdullah Al Mamun et al.. TRIAGE: Risk-Controlled Pseudo-Label Admission for Annotation-Efficient Semi-Supervised Retinal OCT Classification. arXiv. 2026 preprint

TRIAGE addresses the challenge of automating retinal disease diagnosis from optical coherence tomography scans, where annotation costs are high because labeling requires specialist expertise. The paper proposes a semi-supervised learning framework that departs from conventional confidence-based pseudo-label selection by incorporating an asymmetric cost matrix that treats different types of classification errors unequally, reflecting the clinical reality that under-grading disease severity is more dangerous than over-grading. The framework combines a hierarchical classifier that can accommodate partial supervision of disease subtypes, a patient-grouped conformal risk controller using primal-dual optimization for coverage guarantees, and a context-aware Transformer teacher that performs cross-slice verification across volumetric scans. Evaluated on a hospital-derived dataset with unseen test patients, TRIAGE achieves strong scan-level accuracy and macro-level classification metrics using only a small fraction of labeled data, substantially reducing under-grading rates compared to fixed-threshold semi-supervised baselines. The framework generalizes to additional multi-class OCT datasets, maintaining high accuracy even when labeled data is reduced to as little as one percent.

AppleScab-LT: A Longitudinal Real-Field Apple Scab Dataset for Temporal Disease Progression Analysis

Aamir Hilal, Shabir Ahmad Sofi, Neeraj Goel. AppleScab-LT: A Longitudinal Real-Field Apple Scab Dataset for Temporal Disease Progression Analysis. arXiv. 2026 preprint

This paper introduces AppleScab-LT, a longitudinal dataset designed to capture the temporal progression of apple scab disease under natural field conditions, addressing the lack of time-series data in existing plant disease image repositories. The researchers conducted systematic orchard monitoring, repeatedly tracking and photographing individually identified infected leaves across multiple observation points, then applied expert-guided disease verification, polygon-based annotation, and rigorous quality assurance protocols throughout dataset curation. The resulting dataset comprises longitudinal leaf sequences, thousands of high-resolution images, and progressive temporal samples that capture variability in disease severity accumulation, progression rates, and inter-leaf differences. Quantitative disease descriptors based on pixel severity, color-intensity severity, and normalized relative severity are included to provide standardized measurements for temporal analysis. The authors position AppleScab-LT as a foundational resource for disease progression modelling, temporal disease intelligence, and precision agriculture applications.
